package. We did not find qualitative differences between the fits.

S4
Proofs

S4.1
Setup and complete-data likelihood

We first collect the basic notation, conventions, and the complete-data likelihood used throughout.

All vector norms
\|\cdot\|
are Euclidean. Throughout we work with predictable versions of the component intensities
\tau\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-};\theta)
that are left continuous in
t
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{S}
, jointly measurable in
(t,x)
, and continuous in
\theta
. Left continuity is used whenever prejump values are evaluated at event times; when we write
\lambda(\gamma_{i})
we mean
\lambda(t_{i}-,x_{i})
.

We fix
D=(t^{\ast},T]\times\mathcal{S}
with product base measure
\mathrm{d}\tau=\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
and volume

|D|=(T-t^{\ast})\mu(\mathcal{S})
. Throughout, the deterministic observation and schedule windows are open on the left and closed on the right (e.g.
D=(t^{\ast},T]\times\mathcal{S}
). When defining local history neighbourhoods intended to enter an intensity at time
t
, we take them to be open at the right endpoint (i.e. they exclude
t
), e.g.
(t-L,t)\times B_{R}(x)
, to preserve predictability.

We assume
\mu(\mathcal{S})<\infty
, hence
|D|<\infty
. All statements that refer to “large
|D|
” are in a deterministic triangular-array sense: we consider a sequence of windows
D_{n}=(t^{\ast}_{n},T_{n}]\times\mathcal{S}_{n}
with
|D_{n}|\to\infty
and (unless explicitly stated) all constants in assumptions are uniform along the sequence. For readability we drop the subscript
n
.

When discussing high-signal regimes (for example when verifying the concentration Assumption
S4.2
), we also allow the data-generating parameter to vary along the triangular array:

\theta^{\star}=\theta^{\star}_{n}
(hence
P_{\theta^{\star}}=P_{\theta^{\star}_{n}}
), with
\theta^{\star}_{n}\in\Theta_{\circ}
for all
n
, and with the constants in our assumptions chosen uniformly in
n
. We continue to suppress the subscript unless the dependence matters (e.g. for stationary laws, we write
\pi=\pi_{|D|}
). The parameter dimension
p
is fixed (does not grow with
|D|
). Although the point pattern on
D=(t^{\ast},T]\times\mathcal{S}
is observed offline, the finite-sample analysis will work with a time-respecting update schedule to ensure predictability of the integrands used in the martingale bounds.

Fix a deterministic partition

\displaystyle t^{\ast}=

\displaystyle u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{\mathfrak{T}}=T,

\displaystyle D_{m}:=

\displaystyle(u_{m-1},u_{m}]\times\mathcal{S},

\displaystyle D^{[u]}:=(

\displaystyle t^{\ast},u]\times\mathcal{S},

\displaystyle D^{(m)}:=

\displaystyle D^{[u_{m}]}.

Write
N^{[u]}:=N(D^{[u]})
and
N^{(m)}:=N(D^{(m)})
.
We continue to denote the events on
D
by
\gamma_{i}=(t_{i},x_{i})
,

i=1,\dots,N(D)
, ordered by increasing
t_{i}
; then
N^{[u]}=\max\{i:\ t_{i}\leq u\}
. With the convention
\max\emptyset=0
, we have
N^{[t^{\ast}]}=0
and
D^{(0)}=D^{[u_{0}]}=\emptyset
.
Moreover, for
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
,

|D^{[u]}|=(u-t^{\ast})\,\mu(\mathcal{S}),\qquad|D_{m}|=(u_{m}-u_{m-1})\,\mu(\mathcal{S}),\qquad|D^{(m)}|=|D^{[u_{m}]}|.

Throughout the remainder of this section we work on the inference window
D=(t^{\ast},T]\times\mathcal{S}
and use the shifted ground counting process

N^{D}(t):=N\big((t^{\ast},t]\times\mathcal{S}\big),\qquad t\in[t^{\ast},T],

with the convention
N^{D}(t^{\ast})=0
(hence also
N^{D}(t^{\ast}-)=0
). For notational simplicity we suppress the superscript
D
and write
N(t)
and
N(t-)
in place of
N^{D}(t)
and
N^{D}(t-)
. Note that for
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
,

N^{[u]}=N(D^{[u]})=N\big((t^{\ast},u]\times\mathcal{S}\big)=N^{D}(u).

We view
(N_{0},N_{1})
(and hence
N=N_{0}+N_{1}
) as defined on
(-\infty,T]\times\mathcal{S}
.
In addition to the inference window
D=(t^{\ast},T]\times\mathcal{S}
, we assume that the restriction of
N
to a history interval of length
L
immediately preceding
t^{\ast}
is observed and treated as fixed conditioning information. Concretely, throughout we assume that
N
is observed on
(t^{\ast}-L,t^{\ast}]\times\mathcal{S}
and we condition on this observed history. (If one prefers to work with an observation start time
0
, one may shift time so that
t^{\ast}-L\geq 0
, equivalently
t^{\ast}\geq L
.) All likelihoods and intensities on
D
are understood as conditional on this observed history. In particular, whenever a neighbourhood
(t-L,t)\times B_{R}(x)
reaches below
t^{\ast}
, the count is evaluated using the observed pre-
t^{\ast}
history.

Remark S4.1
(Pre-
t^{\ast}
history in component-dependent models)

The preceding conditioning convention is sufficient for the model-agnostic setup, but for specializations in which the component intensities depend on component-specific pre-
t^{\ast}
history (notably Hawkes models), conditioning only on the superposed history
N
before
t^{\ast}
is not enough to identify the oracle intensities
\lambda_{k}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)
. In those specializations we therefore work either under empty pre-history or conditional on the marked pre-
t^{\ast}
history
(N_{0},N_{1})
over the memory window; see §
S5.1
.

We observe an unlabelled simple point process
N=N_{0}+N_{1}
on
D
with simple components
N_{0},N_{1}
. The observed filtration
\{\mathcal{H}_{t}\}
is generated by
N
. The oracle filtration
\{\mathcal{G}_{t}\}
augments
\{\mathcal{H}_{t}\}
with the true component labels
r^{\star}=(r^{\star}_{i})_{i=1}^{N(D)}
, so that
N_{0},N_{1}
are adapted to
\{\mathcal{G}_{t}\}
. We work with predictable versions with respect to the filtration in force and we always assume

\mathcal{H}_{t}\ \subset\ \mathcal{G}_{t}\qquad\text{for every }t,

so
\mathcal{H}
-predictable processes are also
\mathcal{G}
-predictable. We work under the probability law
P_{\theta^{\star}}
on which
N
has
\mathcal{G}
-predictable conditional intensity
\lambda^{\star}
.

Remark S4.2
(Model vs. observed vs. algorithmic objects)

Before proceeding, we note three related objects. (i) Model: the marked process
(N_{0},N_{1})
, the oracle filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{t})
, and the oracle intensities

\lambda_{k}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)
. (ii) Observed process: the superposed process
N=N_{0}+N_{1}
and the observed filtration
(\mathcal{H}_{t})
. (iii) Algorithm/analysis: label sequences
r
, induced counts
N_{k}^{r}
, and label-induced intensities

\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\cdot;\theta)
, which are constructed from
(\mathcal{H}_{t})
and past labels only
(Assumption
S4.2
).

Write
\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau):=\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta^{\star})
and
\lambda^{\star}:=\lambda_{0}^{\star}+\lambda_{1}^{\star}
. For any predictable set
S\subset D
, define the component compensated martingales

M_{k}^{\star}(S)\ :=\ N_{k}(S)-\int_{S}\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau,\qquad k\in\{0,1\},

and the superposed compensated martingale

M^{\star}(S)\ :=\ N(S)-\int_{S}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\ =\ M_{0}^{\star}(S)+M_{1}^{\star}(S).

Throughout the analysis, we index complete-data likelihoods and intensities by a labelling

r=(r_{i})_{i\geq 1}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}.

Only the first
N(D)
entries affect likelihood terms. For the per-flip algebra we may treat
r
as an arbitrary fixed sequence. For the algorithmic update path
(r^{(m)})_{m\leq\mathfrak{T}}
we allow
r^{(m)}
to be random but require it to satisfy the blockwise non-anticipation condition in Assumption
S4.2
below, which ensures that all label-induced intensities used inside martingale integrals on the relevant cumulative windows
D^{(m)}
are predictable (see the definition of
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}
below).

For the analysed update path
(r^{(m)})_{m=0}^{\mathfrak{T}}
we denote the (random) set of visited labellings by

\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}:=\big\{r^{(m)}:\ m=0,1,\dots,\mathfrak{T}\big\}.

When single-event perturbations of an iterate labelling are needed (e.g. in per-flip arguments),
we write the (optional) single-flip closure

\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}^{\rm flip}:=\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}\ \cup\ \big\{\big(r^{(m)}\big)^{(\gamma_{j})}:\ m=0,1,\dots,\mathfrak{T},\ j\leq N^{(m)}\big\},

where
(r^{(m)})^{(\gamma_{j})}
denotes the labelling obtained by flipping only the
j
th coordinate of
r^{(m)}

(as in Lemma
S4.48
).

Throughout we assume the parameter space
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
is nonempty, and we fix a nonempty, compact and convex subset
\Theta_{\circ}\subset\Theta
. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, maximisations are over
\Theta_{\circ}
.

For the M-step it is convenient to introduce an explicit maximiser correspondence.
For any nonempty compact convex set
K\subseteq\Theta_{\circ}
and any labelling
r
, define

\operatorname{ArgMax}_{K}(r)\ :=\ \arg\max_{\theta\in K}\ell_{r}(\theta),\qquad\hat{\theta}_{K}(r)\ \in\ \operatorname{ArgMax}_{K}(r),

where
\hat{\theta}_{K}(r)
denotes an arbitrary (fixed) measurable selection from the nonempty set

\operatorname{ArgMax}_{K}(r)
.

For
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
we also use the restricted correspondence

\operatorname{ArgMax}_{K}^{[u]}(r)\ :=\ \arg\max_{\theta\in K}\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta),\qquad\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r)\ \in\ \operatorname{ArgMax}_{K}^{[u]}(r),

where
\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r)
denotes an arbitrary (fixed) measurable selection.
When the feasible set is
K=\Theta_{\rm sc}
we write
\hat{\theta}_{r}^{[u]}:=\hat{\theta}_{\Theta_{\rm sc}}^{[u]}(r)
.

In the local-curvature regime of Assumption
S4.2
we will also use the localised neighbourhood

\Theta_{\rm sc}:=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc}).

When we write
\hat{\theta}_{r}
without specifying
K
, we mean the localised choice

\hat{\theta}_{r}:=\hat{\theta}_{\Theta_{\rm sc}}(r)
. This localization is imposed only for the analysis (to remain inside the curvature region); see Lemma
S4.68
for conditions under which maximising over
\Theta_{\circ}
and over
\Theta_{\rm sc}
coincide.

We assume throughout that the true parameter lies in the constrained set, i.e.
\theta^{\star}\in\Theta_{\circ}
. When we invoke an unconstrained first-order condition at an M-step maximiser, we will do so by requiring the maximiser
\hat{\theta}_{r}
to lie in the interior of the relevant feasible set
K\in\{\Theta_{\circ},\Theta_{\rm sc}\}
(cf. Assumption
S4.2
); otherwise we use the variational inequality form.

For
k\in\{0,1\}
and for a given history
\mathcal{F}_{t-}\in\{\mathcal{H}_{t-},\mathcal{G}_{t-}\}
possibly augmented by past labels (defined below), denote the predictable component intensity by

\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-};\theta)\qquad(\tau=(t,x)\in D),

and set
\lambda=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}
. We assume
\theta\mapsto\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-};\theta)
is continuous for each
\tau
.

A labelling is a sequence
r=(r_{i})_{i\geq 1}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Define the selected-component process on
[t^{\ast},T)
by

\mathrm{sel}_{r}(t):=r_{N(t-)+1}\qquad(t\in[t^{\ast},T)).

(9)

This agrees with the pre-event label at each event time. For two labellings
r,r^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, define the (window-restricted) Hamming distance

d_{H}(r,r^{\prime}):=\sum_{i=1}^{N(D)}\mathbf{1}\{r_{i}\neq r_{i}^{\prime}\}.

Only the first
N(D)
entries matter throughout. For
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
we also define the

u
-restricted Hamming distance

d_{H}^{[u]}(r,r^{\prime}):=\sum_{i:\,t_{i}\leq u}\mathbf{1}\{r_{i}\neq r_{i}^{\prime}\},\qquad\text{so that}\qquad d_{H}(r,r^{\prime})=d_{H}^{[T]}(r,r^{\prime}).

In what follows, all predictable integrands are constructed using only past labels
r_{1:N(t-)}
on the relevant (cumulative) window; see the definition of
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}
below.

Lemma S4.3
(Predictability of the selected component)

Fix a deterministic labelling
r
. Then
t\mapsto\mathrm{sel}_{r}(t)
is
\{\mathcal{H}_{t-}\}
- and
\{\mathcal{G}_{t-}\}
-predictable on
[t^{\ast},T)
and, at event times
t_{i}<T
,
\mathrm{sel}_{r}(t_{i}-)=r_{i}
.

Proof S4.4
.

Since
N
is adapted, càdlàg, and simple,
t\mapsto N(t-)
is predictable and takes integer values with unit jumps. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the set
\{(t,\omega):N(t-,\omega)=m\}
is predictable, hence the indicator
t\mapsto\mathbbm{1}\{N(t-)=m\}
is predictable. On
[t^{\ast},T)
we have the pointwise identity with pairwise disjoint predictable supports

\mathrm{sel}_{r}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}r_{m+1}\mathbbm{1}\{N(t-)=m\}.

Because the supports are disjoint and for each
(\omega,t)
exactly one indicator equals
1
, this is a countable pointwise sum of predictable indicators; hence it is predictable. Simplicity implies
N(t_{i}-)=i-1
, whence
\mathrm{sel}_{r}(t_{i}-)=r_{i}
.

Remark S4.5

(On predictability of
\mathrm{sel}_{r}
for random iterate labels)
.

Lemma
S4.1
treats deterministic labellings
r
. For random iterate labellings
r^{(m)}
, the process
t\mapsto\mathrm{sel}_{r^{(m)}}(t)=r^{(m)}_{N(t-)+1}
need not be
\mathcal{G}
-predictable. In what follows,
\mathrm{sel}_{r}
is only used inside pathwise algebraic identities, and any resulting selection-remainder term is dominated by stochastic integrals with
\mathcal{G}
-predictable integrands built from past labels
r_{1:N(t-)}
.

For
\tau=(t,x)
and a labelling
r
, define the label-induced intensities using only past labels by

\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta):=\lambda_{k}\!\big(\tau\mid\mathcal{H}_{t-}\vee\sigma(r_{1:N(t-)});\theta\big),\qquad\widehat{\lambda}^{r}:=\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{r}+\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{r}.

If
r
is deterministic then
\sigma(r_{1:N(t-)})
is contained in
\sigma(N(t-))\subset\mathcal{H}_{t-}
(it is not generally trivial because the random length
N(t-)
is
\mathcal{H}_{t-}
-measurable); hence adjoining
\sigma(r_{1:N(t-)})
does not change measurability, and
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\cdot;\theta)
are
\mathcal{H}_{t-}
- and
\mathcal{G}_{t-}
-predictable. More generally, along the update path we assume the blockwise non-anticipation condition of Assumption
S4.2
; in that case for each update
m
and each
t\in[t^{\ast},u_{m})
the vector of past labels
(r^{(m)}_{1},\ldots,r^{(m)}_{N(t-)})
is
\mathcal{G}_{t-}
-measurable and
\mathcal{H}_{t-}\vee\sigma(r^{(m)}_{1:N(t-)})\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{t-}
, so
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r^{(m)}}(\cdot;\theta)
are
\mathcal{G}_{t-}
-predictable on
D^{(m)}
(and, more generally, on any
D^{[u]}
with
u\leq u_{m}
).
All intensities are left-continuous at event times, and every stochastic integral below is evaluated at pre-jump times.

For a labelling
r
and measurable
A\subset\mathcal{S}
,

\widehat{N}_{k}^{r}\big((t_{1},t_{2}]\times A\big):=\sum_{i:t_{1}<t_{i}\leq t_{2},x_{i}\in A}\mathbf{1}\{r_{i}=k\},\qquad k\in\{0,1\}.

By convention, only past labels
r_{1:N(t-)}
enter any predictable integrand at time
t
. For notational convenience, in sections where the true component counts
N_{k}
are not in play we sometimes write
N_{k}^{r}:=\widehat{N}_{k}^{r}
for the counting measure induced by the labelling
r
; we always reserve
N_{k}
(without a
superscript) for the true component process.

For a candidate labelling
r
, the complete-data log-likelihood is

\ell_{r}(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{N(D)}\log\widehat{\lambda}_{r_{i}}^{r}\!\big(\gamma_{i};\theta\big)-\int_{D}\sum_{k=0}^{1}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\mathrm{d}\tau,\qquad f_{r}(\theta):=|D|^{-1}\ell_{r}(\theta).

(10)

For
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
define the restricted complete-data log-likelihood and its normalised version by

\displaystyle\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i:\,t_{i}\leq u}\log\widehat{\lambda}_{r_{i}}^{r}\!\big(\gamma_{i};\theta\big)-\int_{D^{[u]}}\sum_{k=0}^{1}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\mathrm{d}\tau,

(11)

\displaystyle f_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=|D^{[u]}|^{-1}\,\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta).

When
u=T
these coincide with (
10
). When
u=t^{\ast}
we have
D^{[u]}=\emptyset
and
\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)=0
(a.s.); the normalised quantity

f_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)=|D^{[u]}|^{-1}\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)
is only used for
u>t^{\ast}
.

For
\theta\in\Theta
define the oracle and label-induced log-likelihood ratios

s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau):=\log\frac{\lambda_{1}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)}{\lambda_{0}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)},\qquad\tilde{s}_{\theta}(\tau;r):=\log\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{r}(\tau;\theta)}{\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{r}(\tau;\theta)}.

Given
b>0
, the decisive sets and ambiguous band are

S_{\theta}^{+}(b)=\{\tau:\ s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)\geq b\},\quad S_{\theta}^{-}(b)=\{\tau:\ s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)\leq-b\},\quad A_{\theta}(b)=\{\tau:\ |s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)|\leq b\}.

Since
s_{\theta}^{\rm or}
is
\mathcal{G}
-predictable, the indicator processes
(\omega,\tau)\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{S_{\theta}^{\pm}(b)}(\omega,\tau)
and
(\omega,\tau)\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{A_{\theta}(b)}(\omega,\tau)
are
\mathcal{G}
-predictable.

Definition S4.6

(Minority label on decisive sets)
.

Fix
\theta
and
b>0
. For an event
\gamma_{i}
:

\text{minority}(\gamma_{i};\theta,b)=\begin{cases}0,&\gamma_{i}\in S_{\theta}^{+}(b),\\
1,&\gamma_{i}\in S_{\theta}^{-}(b),\\
\text{undefined},&\gamma_{i}\notin S_{\theta}^{+}(b)\cup S_{\theta}^{-}(b).\end{cases}

Remark S4.7

(Oracle-aided viewpoint)
.

The penalty below uses the oracle decisive sets
S_{\theta}^{\pm}(b)
; it is therefore not implementable from observed data. We analyse the resulting oracle-aided hard–EM purely to derive sufficient structural conditions for contraction and consistency.

S4.2
Model-agnostic structural assumptions

For each window
D
, fix a radius
\varepsilon_{\rm net}\leq|D|^{-1}
and an
\varepsilon_{\rm net}
-net
\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}\subset\Theta_{\circ}
; let
N_{\rm net}:=|\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}|
.
Since the parameter dimension
p
is fixed and
\Theta_{\circ}
is compact,
N_{\rm net}
grows at most polynomially in
|D|
; in particular
\log N_{\rm net}=O(\log|D|)
. Throughout we write

z_{\rm Fr}:=\log\!\Big(c_{0}(1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|\mathfrak{T}(p+2)N_{\rm net}\Big)+\eta_{x}\log|D|,

(12)

and for concreteness we take
c_{0}=10
and
\eta_{x}=2
. This choice absorbs two-sided Freedman inequality factors, the covering number
N_{\rm net}
, and small constant multipliers in the master union bound over updates, components, coordinates, and net points, as well as similar auxiliary Freedman inequality calls used in the appendices, yielding probabilities at least
1-|D|^{-\eta^{\prime\prime}}
for some
\eta^{\prime\prime}>0
. Since
p
is fixed,
z_{\rm Fr}=\Theta(\log|D|)
.

On
E_{\rm act}
(defined in
Section

S4.2
), the blockwise operator of Algorithm
10
assigns labels progressively and never revises past events. Hence the total number of accepted eventwise label assignments over the full update path is at most
N(D)
, and our union bounds and flip-count accounting refer to those progressive assignments. In the martingale analysis we apply Freedman’s inequality to a finite collection of scalar martingales (e.g. per-event terms and gradient-coordinate terms at M-step anchors) indexed by update/block indices, event indices, components, coordinates, and net points. The factor
\mathfrak{T}
in (
12
) is a conservative envelope that absorbs finite unions over update indices and ancillary Freedman calls (e.g. those used in appendices), in addition to the
O(|D|)
eventwise decisions controlled on
E_{\rm act}
.

To make the Freedman inequality bounds uniform over
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
, we first prove them at each net point
\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}
and then extend to all of
\Theta_{\circ}
using deterministic Lipschitz bounds in
\theta
(coming from the bounded gradients in
Section

S4.2
and the uniform lower margins in
Section

S4.2
). Since
\varepsilon_{\rm net}\leq|D|^{-1}
, the resulting deterministic discretization error is
O(|D|^{-1})
at the
f_{r}
scale and can be absorbed into the residual terms.

Thus the total number of Freedman inequality tail events we union bound over is at most a constant multiple of

(p+2)\,N_{\rm net}\,\sum_{m=1}^{\mathfrak{T}}N^{(m)}.

Since
N^{(m)}\leq N(D)
for every
m
, we have
\sum_{m=1}^{\mathfrak{T}}N^{(m)}\leq\mathfrak{T}\,N(D)
, and on
E_{\rm act}
this is
bounded by
(1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|\,\mathfrak{T}
. Consequently, choosing
z_{\rm Fr}
as in (
12
) (which already
contains the factor
|D|\mathfrak{T}
) makes the overall union bound failure probability polynomially small in
|D|
.

Assumption S4.8

(Activity and tails)

There exists
C_{\rm act}<\infty
and
\eta_{\rm act}\in(0,1)
such that
\mathbb{E}N(D)\leq C_{\rm act}|D|
and

\mathbb{P}\!\big(N(D)\geq(1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|\big)\leq e^{-c|D|}

for some
c>0
and all sufficiently large
|D|
. Denote

E_{\rm act}:=\big\{N(D)\leq(1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|\big\}.

Assumption S4.9

(Predictable blockwise updates)

Fix a deterministic partition
t^{\ast}=u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{\mathfrak{T}}=T
as in §
S4.1
.
Along the update path
\{(r^{(m)},\theta^{(m)}):m=0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}\}
the following hold:

(a)

(Parameter timing)
For each
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}\}
, the update parameter

\theta^{(m)}
is
\mathcal{G}_{u_{m}}
-measurable.

(b)

(Progressive, non-revisiting labels)
For each
m\in\{1,\dots,\mathfrak{T}\}
and each event index
i
with

t_{i}\leq u_{m}
, the label
r_{i}^{(m)}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable, and labels are never revised:
for all
\ell\geq m
,
r_{i}^{(\ell)}=r_{i}^{(m)}
for every
i
with
t_{i}\leq u_{m}
.
In particular, for each
m
the vector
(r_{1}^{(m)},\dots,r_{N^{(m)}}^{(m)})
is
\mathcal{G}_{u_{m}}
-measurable.

In particular, for every
m
and every
t\in[t^{\ast},u_{m})
the vector of past labels

(r^{(m)}_{1},\ldots,r^{(m)}_{N(t-)})
is
\mathcal{G}_{t-}
-measurable and hence

\mathcal{H}_{t-}\vee\sigma(r^{(m)}_{1:N(t-)})\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{G}_{t-}.

Consequently, for every
m
, every
u\leq u_{m}
, and every
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
,
the label-induced intensities
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r^{(m)}}(\cdot;\theta)
are
\mathcal{G}_{t-}
-predictable on
D^{[u]}
.

Assumption S4.10

(Uniform margins over labellings)

There exist
\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}>0
and
\underline{\mu}_{k}>0
(
k=0,1
) such that, for a.e.
\tau\in D
, all
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
, and all deterministic labellings
r
,

\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{r}(\tau;\theta)+\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\ \geq\ \underline{\mu}_{\Sigma},\qquad\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\ \geq\ \underline{\mu}_{k}.

Write
\underline{\mu}_{\min}:=\min\{\underline{\mu}_{0},\underline{\mu}_{1}\}
.

Assumption S4.11

(Oracle positivity)

There exist
\underline{\lambda}_{k}>0
such that
\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)\geq\underline{\lambda}_{k}
for all
\tau\in D
,
k\in\{0,1\}
, and
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
. Hence
s_{\theta}^{\rm or}=\log(\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{0})
is well-defined.

Assumption S4.12

(Window envelope along the iterate path)

There exist deterministic model constants
L>0
(temporal memory),
R\geq 0
(spatial radius;
R=0
if not applicable), and
C_{\rm base},C_{\rm loc}<\infty
(depending only on the model class and
\Theta_{\circ}
). For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the window-count event

\Omega_{n}(L,R):=\left\{\ \sup_{\tau=(t,x)\in D}\ N\Big((t-L,t)\times B_{R}(x)\Big)\ \leq\ n\ \right\}.

There exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
and an exponent
\eta_{\rm win}>0
such that, with
n_{*}(|D|):=\lceil c_{1}\log|D|\rceil
,

\mathbb{P}\big(\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)^{c}\big)\ \leq\ c_{2}|D|^{-\eta_{\rm win}}.

(13)

On the event
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
we have, simultaneously for all
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
, for the oracle intensities
\lambda^{\star}
and for all labellings
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}^{\rm flip}
,

\sup_{\tau\in D}\ \big(\lambda_{0}^{\star}+\lambda_{1}^{\star}\big)(\tau)\ \leq\ K_{\rm win},\qquad\sup_{\tau\in D}\ \big(\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{r}+\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{r}\big)(\tau;\theta)\ \leq\ K_{\rm win},

where

K_{\rm win}:=C_{\rm base}+C_{\rm loc}n_{*}(|D|).

In particular, on
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
we have
K_{\rm win}=O(\log|D|)
.

Remark S4.13
.

In Assumption
S4.2
we only require the existence of
\eta_{\rm win}>0
such that
Equation

13
holds, thereby providing a polynomially small tail for the window event. This can then be combined with the martingale errors that are controlled via Freedman’s inequality. In particular, the exponent
\eta_{x}>1
that appears in the definition of the Freedman inequality level
z_{\rm Fr}
is not constrained by
\eta_{\rm win}
.

Assumption S4.14

(Single-flip locality and influence)

For any single flip at an event location
\zeta
, with
\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}=\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}-\widehat{\lambda}^{r}
and
\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}=\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r^{\prime}}-\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}
, there exist finite constants

B_{\infty},\ B_{1},\ B_{2},\qquad B_{\infty}^{\rm comp},\ B_{1}^{\rm comp},\ B_{2}^{\rm comp},\qquad B_{1}^{\Sigma},\ B_{2}^{\Sigma}

such that

\|\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}\|_{\infty}\leq B_{\infty},\quad\int_{D}|\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}|\mathrm{d}\tau\leq B_{1},\quad\int_{D}\frac{(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r})^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq B_{2},

and, for
k\in\{0,1\}
,

\|\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}\|_{\infty}\leq B_{\infty}^{\rm comp},\quad\int_{D}|\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}|\mathrm{d}\tau\leq B_{1}^{\rm comp},\quad\int_{D}\frac{(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq B_{2}^{\rm comp},

as well as
\sum_{k}\int_{D}|\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}|\mathrm{d}\tau\leq B_{1}^{\Sigma}
and
\sum_{k}\int_{D}\frac{(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq B_{2}^{\Sigma}
.

In addition (and used when
\nabla\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
denominators appear), there exist finite constants
\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\rm comp}<\infty
and
\widetilde{B}_{2}^{\Sigma}<\infty
such that, for each
k
,

\int_{D}\frac{(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\ \leq\ \widetilde{B}_{2}^{\rm comp},\qquad\sum_{k=0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\ \leq\ \widetilde{B}_{2}^{\Sigma}.

In terms of the gradient-level analogues, there exist finite constants

\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\rm comp},\ \overline{B}_{1}^{\rm comp},\ \overline{B}_{2}^{\rm comp},\qquad\overline{B}_{1}^{\Sigma}

such that, for each component
k
,

\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})\|_{\infty}\leq\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\rm comp},\quad\int_{D}\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})\|\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\overline{B}_{1}^{\rm comp},\quad\int_{D}\frac{\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})\|^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\overline{B}_{2}^{\rm comp},

and
\sum_{k}\int_{D}\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})\|\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\overline{B}_{1}^{\Sigma}
.

Define the total-gradient difference
\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}^{r}):=\sum_{k=0}^{1}\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})
. Then, for notational convenience, introduce finite constants
\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
and
\overline{B}_{2}^{\Sigma}
such that

\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}^{r})\|_{\infty}\leq\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\Sigma},\qquad\int_{D}\frac{\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}^{r})\|^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\overline{B}_{2}^{\Sigma}.

For instance, one may take
\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}:=2\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\rm comp}
and
\overline{B}_{2}^{\Sigma}:=4\overline{B}_{2}^{\rm comp}
.

Furthermore, there exists
S_{\infty}<\infty
and
H_{\infty}<\infty
such that

\operatorname*{esssup}_{\tau,k,\theta,r}\ \|\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\|\ \leq\ S_{\infty},\qquad\operatorname*{esssup}_{\tau,k,\theta,r}\ \|\nabla^{2}_{\theta\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\|\ \leq\ H_{\infty}.

The same bounds hold when
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)
is replaced by the oracle intensity

\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)
.

Finally, there exist finite constants
\widetilde{\overline{B}}_{2}^{\rm comp}<\infty
such that, for each
k
,

\int_{D}\frac{\|\Delta(\nabla_{\theta}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r})\|^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\ \leq\ \widetilde{\overline{B}}_{2}^{\rm comp}.

Assumption S4.15

(Uniform score alignment)

There exist a deterministic sequence
\Delta_{s}(|D|)\geq 0
, an exponent
\eta>0
, and events

E^{\rm align}_{|D|}
with
\mathbb{P}(E^{\rm align}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
such that, on
E^{\rm align}_{|D|}
, uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
, all deterministic labellings
r
, and a.e.
\tau\in D
,

\big|\tilde{s}_{\theta}(\tau;r)-s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)\big|\ \leq\ \Delta_{s}(|D|).

For brevity, on
E^{\rm align}_{|D|}
we write
\Delta_{s}:=\Delta_{s}(|D|)
.

Remark S4.16

(Consequence on decisive sets)
.

On the event
E^{\rm align}_{|D|}
, Assumption
S4.2
implies that, for any
b>0
,

\tau\in S_{\theta}^{+}(b)\Rightarrow\tilde{s}_{\theta}(\tau;r)\geq b-\Delta_{s},\qquad\tau\in S_{\theta}^{-}(b)\Rightarrow\tilde{s}_{\theta}(\tau;r)\leq-b+\Delta_{s}.

In particular, if
b\geq\Delta_{s}
then
\tilde{s}_{\theta}(\cdot;r)
and
s_{\theta}^{\rm or}(\cdot)
have the same sign
on
S_{\theta}^{+}(b)\cup S_{\theta}^{-}(b)
.

Assumption S4.17

(LLR Lipschitz in
\theta
(average-case))

There exist a deterministic sequence
L_{s}(|D|)<\infty
, an
\eta>0
, and events
E^{\rm Lip}_{|D|}
with
\mathbb{P}(E^{\rm Lip}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
such that, for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\rm sc}
,

\int_{D}|s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)-s^{\rm or}_{\theta^{\prime}}(\tau)|\,\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\,d\tau\leq L_{s}(|D|)\,|D|\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|.

Moreover, on the same event
E^{\rm Lip}_{|D|}
the bound holds uniformly on the deterministic schedule subwindows:
for every
m\in\{1,\dots,\mathfrak{T}\}
and all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\rm sc}
,

\displaystyle\int_{D^{(m)}}\big|s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)-s^{\rm or}_{\theta^{\prime}}(\tau)\big|\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\ \leq

\displaystyle\ L_{s}(|D|)\,|D^{(m)}|\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|,

\displaystyle\int_{D_{m}}\big|s^{\rm or}_{\theta}(\tau)-s^{\rm or}_{\theta^{\prime}}(\tau)\big|\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\ \leq

\displaystyle\ L_{s}(|D|)\,|D_{m}|\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|.

On the event
E^{\rm Lip}_{|D|}
in Assumption
S4.2
, we write
L_{s}:=L_{s}(|D|)
for brevity.

Assumption S4.18

(Local strong concavity & near-zero score at
r^{\star}
)

There exist constants
r_{\rm sc}>0
,
m_{\rm sc}>0
,
C_{\rm sc}<\infty
, and
\eta>0
such that, for all sufficiently large
|D|
, there is an event
E^{\rm sc}_{|D|}
with
\mathbb{P}(E^{\rm sc}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
on which:

(a)

(Uniform local curvature)

f_{r^{\star}}
is differentiable on

\Theta_{\rm sc}:=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc}),\qquad B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc}):=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\ \|\theta-\theta^{\star}\|\leq r_{\rm sc}\},

and satisfies the gradient monotonicity inequality

\big(\nabla f_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\prime})-\nabla f_{r^{\star}}(\theta)\big)^{\top}(\theta^{\prime}-\theta)\ \leq\ -m_{\rm sc}\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|^{2}\qquad\forall\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\rm sc}.

(14)

If
f_{r^{\star}}
is twice differentiable, a sufficient condition for (
14
) is the uniform Hessian bound

-\nabla^{2}f_{r^{\star}}(\theta)\succeq m_{\rm sc}I_{p}
for all
\theta\in\Theta_{\rm sc}
.

(b)

(Score at the truth)

\|\nabla f_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})\|\ \leq\ C_{\rm sc}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{K_{\rm win}z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}}+\frac{z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}\right)\!,

where
K_{\rm win}
is as in
Section

S4.2
. In bounded-intensity settings, the score bound in (b) follows from Lemma
S4.36
on
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
by choosing
z_{\rm Fr}\simeq\log|D|
(and using
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)^{c})\leq c_{2}|D|^{-\eta_{\rm win}}
from Assumption
S4.2
).

(c)

(Uniformity over deterministic prefixes)

For every
m\in\{1,\dots,\mathfrak{T}\}
, the conclusions in (a) and (b) also hold with the full-window objective

f_{r^{\star}}
replaced by the prefix objective
f_{r^{\star}}^{[u_{m}]}
, with the same constants

r_{\rm sc},m_{\rm sc},C_{\rm sc}
, and with
|D|
replaced by
|D^{(m)}|=|D^{[u_{m}]}|
in the score bound.

Further, assume
\theta^{\star}
lies in a compact subset of
\Theta_{\circ}
bounded away from any non-identifiability/collision boundary. Equivalently, there exists
\delta_{\rm sep}>0
such that
\mathrm{dist}(\theta^{\star},\partial\Theta_{\rm id})\geq\delta_{\rm sep}
,
and choose
r_{\rm sc}\leq\delta_{\rm sep}/2
.

Remark S4.19

(On concavity restrictions)
.

In Hawkes models, global log-likelihood concavity typically holds for parameters that enter linearly (e.g. baselines and excitation amplitudes) but can fail for nonlinear parameters such as the decay rate
\beta
. Accordingly, rather than imposing global concavity on
\Theta_{\circ}
, we use the local condition in Assumption
S4.2
: it suffices that
f_{r^{\star}}
is uniformly strongly concave on a deterministic-radius neighborhood
\Theta_{\rm sc}=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc})
, and we then keep all iterates inside
\Theta_{\rm sc}
via an invariant-set argument.

Assumption S4.20

(Warm start)

The initialization satisfies
\|\theta^{(0)}-\theta^{\star}\|\leq r_{\rm sc}
(equivalently
\theta^{(0)}\in\Theta_{\rm sc}
).

Remark S4.21

(Why Assumption
S4.2
is reasonable)
.

This warm-start condition motivates the requirement that the process/history up to
t^{\star}
is observed: if one can construct a pilot estimator
\tilde{\theta}_{0}
from the data on
[0,t^{\star}]\times\mathcal{S}
such that
\tilde{\theta}_{0}
is consistent for the initial parameter
\theta_{0}^{\star}
(and in our setting
\theta_{0}^{\star}=\theta^{\star}
), then for sufficiently large sample size (or amount of data) we have
\|\tilde{\theta}_{0}-\theta^{\star}\|\leq r_{\rm sc}
with high probability. Thus, taking
\theta^{(0)}:=\tilde{\theta}_{0}
makes Assumption
S4.2
operational.

Definition S4.22

(Canonical telescoping path)
.

Given two labellings
r,r^{\star}\in\{0,1\}^{N(D)}
, let

I:=\{i\in\{1,\dots,N(D)\}:\ r_{i}\neq r_{i}^{\star}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}:=|I|
.
Fix a deterministic ordering of
I
(e.g., increasing index), write

I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{\mathfrak{I}})
, and define a sequence
(r^{(j)})_{j=0}^{\mathfrak{I}}
by
r^{(0)}:=r
,

r^{(j)}:=\text{ the labelling obtained from }r^{(j-1)}\text{ by flipping only coordinate }i_{j}\quad(j=1,\ldots,\mathfrak{I}).

We call
(r^{(0)},r^{(1)},\ldots,r^{(\mathfrak{I})})
the canonical telescoping path
from
r
to
r^{\star}
. It has length
\mathfrak{I}=d_{H}(r,r^{\star})
and ends at
r^{(\mathfrak{I})}=r^{\star}
. For any functional
F
on labellings,

F(r)-F(r^{\star})=\sum_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{I}}\big(F(r^{(j-1)})-F(r^{(j)})\big).

Assumption S4.23

(Label-to-score Lipschitz)

There exist constants
\bar{C}_{0},\bar{C}_{1},\bar{C}_{2}<\infty
and
\eta>0
such that, for all large
|D|
, on an event

E^{\nabla}_{|D|}
with
\mathbb{P}(E^{\nabla}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
the following holds simultaneously:

for every update index
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}-1\}
, letting
u:=u_{m+1}
and
r:=r^{(m+1)}
, for every choice of
restricted M-step maximiser

\hat{\theta}_{r}^{[u]}\ \in\ \arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm sc}}\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta),

we have

\left\|\nabla f_{r}^{[u]}(\hat{\theta}_{r}^{[u]})-\nabla f_{r^{\star}}^{[u]}(\hat{\theta}_{r}^{[u]})\right\|\ \leq\ \frac{d_{H}^{[u]}(r,r^{\star})}{|D^{[u]}|}\Big(\bar{C}_{0}K_{\rm win}+\bar{C}_{1}\sqrt{K_{\rm win}z_{\rm Fr}}+\bar{C}_{2}z_{\rm Fr}\Big).

Definition S4.24

(Decisive-set disagreement set)
.

For
b>0
and any
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
, define

B_{\theta}\ :=\ \big(S_{\theta^{\star}}^{+}(b)\Delta S_{\theta}^{+}(b)\big)\ \cup\ \big(S_{\theta^{\star}}^{-}(b)\Delta S_{\theta}^{-}(b)\big).

Assumption S4.25

(Mass on decisive sets and oracle-set concentration)

All expectations and probabilities in this assumption are with respect to
P_{\theta^{\star}}
.
There exist
\eta>0
,
c>0
,
C_{\rm it}>0
, and, for each threshold
b=b_{|D|}>0
used in the theorem under consideration, nonnegative sequences

\eta_{+}(|D|;b),\qquad\eta_{-}(|D|;b),

such that
\mathfrak{T}=\lceil C_{\rm it}\log|D|\rceil
, and the following hold.

(i)

(Minority mass on oracle decisive sets at threshold
b
).

\mathbb{E}N_{0}\!\big(S_{\theta^{\star}}^{+}(b)\big)\ \leq\ \eta_{+}(|D|;b)\,|D|,\qquad\mathbb{E}N_{1}\!\big(S_{\theta^{\star}}^{-}(b)\big)\ \leq\ \eta_{-}(|D|;b)\,|D|.

Since
S_{\theta^{\star}}^{\pm}(b)\subseteq S_{\theta^{\star}}^{\pm}(b_{\rm th})

for
b\geq b_{\rm th}
, the bounds for
b_{\rm th}
imply the same bounds for
b
.

(ii)

(Concentration for fixed oracle sets and iterate-dependent disagreement sets).

Moreover, for all
\delta\in(0,1)
and for
S
ranging over the oracle sets

\mathcal{F}_{\rm fixed}(b):=\big\{S_{\theta^{\star}}^{+}(b),S_{\theta^{\star}}^{-}(b),A_{\theta^{\star}}(b),A_{\theta^{\star}}(2b)\big\},

we have oracle-set concentration simultaneously for total and component counts around their means:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\left|N(S)-\mathbb{E}N(S)\right|\geq\delta|D|\right)\leq

\displaystyle\exp\left(-c\frac{\delta^{2}|D|}{K_{\rm win}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\left|N_{k}(S)-\mathbb{E}N_{k}(S)\right|\geq\delta|D|\right)\leq

\displaystyle\exp\left(-c\frac{\delta^{2}|D|}{K_{\rm win}}\right)

for
k\in\{0,1\}
.

Define the mean-deviation event

\displaystyle E^{\rm mean}_{|D|}

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{\rm fixed}(b)}\left\{\left|N(S)-\mathbb{E}N(S)\right|\leq C_{\rm it}\sqrt{K_{\rm win}|D|\log|D|}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\cap\bigcap_{k=0}^{1}\bigcap_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{\rm fixed}(b)}\left\{\left|N_{k}(S)-\mathbb{E}N_{k}(S)\right|\leq C_{\rm it}\sqrt{K_{\rm win}|D|\log|D|}\right\}.

Assume
\mathbb{P}(E^{\rm mean}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
for some
\eta>0
(finite union over
\mathcal{F}_{\rm fixed}(b)
).

We also require concentration around compensators for the same fixed oracle sets and for the iterate-dependent disagreement sets.
Define
E^{\rm comp}_{|D|}
to be the event on which the following hold simultaneously:

(a)

for all
S\in\mathcal{F}_{\rm fixed}(b)
and all
k\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\Big|N_{k}(S)-\int_{S}\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\Big|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\rm it}\sqrt{K_{\rm win}|D|\log|D|},

\displaystyle\Big|N(S)-\int_{S}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\Big|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\rm it}\sqrt{K_{\rm win}|D|\log|D|};

(b)

for all iterates
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}-1\}
,

\Big|N(B_{\theta^{(m)}}\cap D_{m+1})-\int_{B_{\theta^{(m)}}\cap D_{m+1}}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\Big|\leq C_{\rm it}\sqrt{K_{\rm win}|D_{m+1}|\log|D|}.

Assume
\mathbb{P}(E^{\rm comp}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
for some (possibly different)
\eta>0
. Finally set

E^{\rm mass}_{|D|}\ :=\ E^{\rm mean}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm comp}_{|D|},

and assume
\mathbb{P}(E^{\rm mass}_{|D|})\geq 1-|D|^{-\eta}
for some
\eta>0
.

All expectation and concentration statements above are also assumed to hold uniformly when the observation
window is replaced by any deterministic prefix window
D^{(m)}
or deterministic block
D_{m}
from the schedule.
Equivalently, we may replace any set
S\subset D
appearing above by
S\cap W
and replace
|D|
by
|W|
for any

W\in\{D^{(m)},D_{m}\}
, with the same constants (and with the corresponding
N(W)
-type counts).
In particular, we include in
E^{\rm comp}_{|D|}
the compensator deviations for the restricted fixed oracle sets
S\cap D_{m}
(for
S\in\mathcal{F}_{\rm fixed}(b)
) and, for iterate-dependent disagreement sets, the blockwise deviations on
B_{\theta^{(m)}}\cap D_{m+1}
as in (b). Since the schedule is deterministic and has size
O(\mathfrak{T})
, these additional requirements are absorbed by a finite union.

Lemma S4.26

(Existence of M-step maximisers)
.

Work on the window-envelope event
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
from Assumption
S4.2
.
Let
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}
be any labelling on the iterate path and let
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
.

(a)

The map
\theta\mapsto\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)
is continuous on
\Theta_{\circ}
and hence attains a maximum on
\Theta_{\circ}
.
Consequently
\operatorname{ArgMax}_{\Theta_{\circ}}^{[u]}(r)
is nonempty.

(b)

Let
\Theta_{\rm sc}:=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc})
as in Assumption
S4.2
and
suppose
\Theta_{\rm sc}\neq\emptyset
.
Then
\theta\mapsto\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)
also attains a maximum on
\Theta_{\rm sc}
, so

\operatorname{ArgMax}_{\Theta_{\rm sc}}^{[u]}(r)
is nonempty.

(c)

Taking
u=T
yields the same conclusions for the full-window objective
\ell_{r}=\ell_{r}^{[T]}
.

Proof S4.27
.

Fix
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
and
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}
.
By Assumption
S4.2
,
\widehat{\lambda}_{r_{i}}^{r}(\gamma_{i};\theta)\geq\underline{\mu}_{\min}
for all
i
and
\theta
,
so
\theta\mapsto\log\widehat{\lambda}_{r_{i}}^{r}(\gamma_{i};\theta)
is continuous.

On
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
, Assumption
S4.2
provides a deterministic bound
K_{\rm win}
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{1}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\leq K_{\rm win}
for all
\tau\in D
, all
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
,
and all
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}
. Since
\theta\mapsto\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)
is continuous for each
\tau
,
dominated convergence (dominated by
K_{\rm win}
on the finite-measure set
D^{[u]}
) implies continuity of

\theta\ \longmapsto\ \int_{D^{[u]}}\sum_{k=0}^{1}\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Therefore
\ell_{r}^{[u]}
is continuous on
\Theta_{\circ}
. Since
\Theta_{\circ}
is compact,

\ell_{r}^{[u]}
attains a maximum on
\Theta_{\circ}
.

The same argument applies on the compact set
\Theta_{\rm sc}=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc})
.
Finally, taking
u=T
recovers
\ell_{r}=\ell_{r}^{[T]}
.

Assumption S4.28

(M-step optimality condition and differentiability)

For every labelling
r
on the iterate path
\mathcal{R}_{\rm path}
and every
u\in[t^{\ast},T]
,
the objective
f_{r}^{[u]}
admits a
C^{1}
extension to an open neighbourhood of
\Theta_{\circ}
.

For any convex constraint set
K\in\{\Theta_{\circ},\Theta_{\rm sc}\}
and any maximiser

\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r)\in\arg\max_{\theta\in K}\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)
, the (global) first-order optimality condition holds:

(\theta-\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r))^{\top}\nabla f_{r}^{[u]}(\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r))\ \leq\ 0\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in K.

In particular, if
\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r)\in\operatorname{int}(K)
then

\nabla f_{r}^{[u]}(\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r))=0
.
Taking
u=T
recovers the same condition for the full-window objective
f_{r}
.

Remark S4.29

(Why the variational inequality in Assumption
S4.2
is automatic)
.

The feasible set
\Theta_{\rm sc}:=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc})
is convex since
\Theta_{\circ}
is convex. Negative definiteness of the Hessian (equivalently, local strong concavity) is only required on
\Theta_{\rm sc}
(Assumption
S4.2
); possible degeneracies elsewhere in
\Theta_{\circ}
are irrelevant provided the iterates remain in
\Theta_{\rm sc}
(Lemma
S4.64
). For any convex constraint set
K
and any maximiser
\hat{\theta}_{K}(r)\in\arg\max_{\theta\in K}\ell_{r}(\theta)
, the standard first-order necessary condition for constrained maximisation gives

(\theta-\hat{\theta}_{K}(r))^{\top}\nabla f_{r}(\hat{\theta}_{K}(r))\leq 0\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in K.

In particular, if
\hat{\theta}_{K}(r)\in\operatorname{int}(K)
then
\nabla f_{r}(\hat{\theta}_{K}(r))=0
.

Remark S4.30

(Optional margin assumption for the band)
.

In some applications, one can assume the oracle margin: there exist
\alpha_{\rm band}>0
and
C_{\rm band}<\infty
such that for all small
u>0
,

\frac{1}{|D|}\int_{D}\mathbbm{1}\{|s_{\theta^{\star}}^{\rm or}(\tau)|\leq u\}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\ \leq\ C_{\rm band}u^{\alpha_{\rm band}}.

Under this assumption and the oracle-set concentration in
Section

S4.2
, with high probability

N(A_{\theta^{\star}}(2b))/|D|\lesssim C_{\rm band}(2b)^{\alpha_{\rm band}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{K_{\rm win}\log|D|}{|D|}}\right)

for those
b
with
2b
in the validity range of the margin bound.

Definition S4.31

(Ambiguous-band mass)
.

For any
b>0
define

C_{b}(\theta^{\star})\ :=\ \frac{1}{|D|}\int_{D}\mathbbm{1}\{|s_{\theta^{\star}}^{\rm or}(\tau)|\leq 2b\}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau.

By
Section

S4.2
, we have
|D|^{-1}N\!\big(A_{\theta^{\star}}(2b)\big)=C_{b}(\theta^{\star})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
as
|D|\to\infty
.

S4.3
Freedman inequality and auxiliary lemmas

Lemma S4.32

(Indicator of a post-stopping-time interval is predictable)
.

Let
\sigma
be a stopping time (with respect to the filtration in force). Then the set
\{(t,\omega):t>\sigma(\omega)\}
is predictable. Equivalently,
t\mapsto\mathbbm{1}\{t>\sigma\}
is predictable.

Proof S4.33
.

Define
X_{t}:=\mathbbm{1}\{t>\sigma\}
. Since
\sigma
is a stopping time,
\{\sigma\leq q\}\in\mathcal{F}_{q}
for all
q
.
Moreover, for each
t
,

\{X_{t}=1\}=\{\sigma<t\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathbb{Q}\\
q<t\end{subarray}}\{\sigma\leq q\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t},

so
(X_{t})
is adapted. Moreover
X
is left-continuous:
X_{\sigma}=0
and for
t\uparrow\sigma
we have
X_{t}=0
. Every adapted left-continuous process is predictable, hence
X
is predictable.

Lemma S4.34

(Freedman inequality for point-process martingales)
.

Let
M^{\star}
be the compensated martingale of
N
and let
h
be
\mathcal{G}
-predictable with
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq H<\infty
. Set

X_{T}=\int_{D}h\,\mathrm{d}M^{\star},\qquad V=\int_{D}h^{2}\lambda^{\star}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Then, for any
z_{\rm Fr}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(X_{T}\geq\frac{H}{3}z_{\rm Fr}+\sqrt{2Vz_{\rm Fr}+\frac{H^{2}}{9}z_{\rm Fr}^{2}}\right)\leq

\displaystyle e^{-z_{\rm Fr}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(|X_{T}|\geq\frac{H}{3}z_{\rm Fr}+\sqrt{2Vz_{\rm Fr}+\frac{H^{2}}{9}z_{\rm Fr}^{2}}\right)\leq

\displaystyle 2e^{-z_{\rm Fr}}.

The same bounds hold when
M^{\star}
is replaced by either component martingale
M_{k}^{\star}
and
\lambda^{\star}
by
\lambda_{k}^{\star}
.

Proof S4.35
.

This is a standard continuous time analogue of Freedman inequality for martingales with bounded jumps and predictable quadratic characteristic
(
15
)
, the properties of which are discussed in depth in
12
.

Lemma S4.36

(Near-zero oracle score at the truth via Freedman)
.

Assume oracle positivity (Assumption
S4.2
) and the oracle gradient bound in
Assumption
S4.2
. Fix
z_{\rm Fr}>0
and work on the window envelope event

\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
of Assumption
S4.2
, so that
\sup_{\tau\in D}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\leq K_{\rm win}
.
Then with probability at least
1-4p\,e^{-z_{\rm Fr}}
(conditional on
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
),

\|\nabla f_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})\|\ \leq\ C_{\rm sc}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{K_{\rm win}z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}}+\frac{z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}\right),

where one may take

C_{\rm sc}\ :=\ \frac{8\sqrt{2p}\,S_{\infty}}{\underline{\lambda}_{\min}},\qquad\underline{\lambda}_{\min}:=\min\{\underline{\lambda}_{0},\underline{\lambda}_{1}\}.

Proof S4.37
.

Write
\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau):=\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta^{\star})
and recall that under
r^{\star}

(the true labels) the complete-data log-likelihood is

\ell_{r^{\star}}(\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(\int_{D}\log\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)\,dN_{k}(\tau)-\int_{D}\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta)\,d\tau\right).

Differentiating and evaluating at
\theta^{\star}
gives

\nabla\ell_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})=\sum_{k=0}^{1}\left(\int_{D}\nabla_{\theta}\log\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta^{\star})\,dN_{k}(\tau)-\int_{D}\nabla_{\theta}\lambda_{k}(\tau\mid\mathcal{G}_{t-};\theta^{\star})\,d\tau\right).

Using the Doob–Meyer decomposition
dN_{k}=dM_{k}^{\star}+\lambda_{k}^{\star}\,d\tau
and the identity

\nabla_{\theta}\log\lambda_{k}=(\nabla_{\theta}\lambda_{k})/\lambda_{k}
, we obtain the cancellation

\int_{D}\nabla_{\theta}\log\lambda_{k}^{\star}\,\lambda_{k}^{\star}\,d\tau=\int_{D}\nabla_{\theta}\lambda_{k}^{\star}\,d\tau,

hence

\nabla\ell_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})=\sum_{k=0}^{1}\int_{D}\nabla_{\theta}\log\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\,dM_{k}^{\star}(\tau).

(15)

Dividing by
|D|
yields the same representation for
\nabla f_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})
.

Fix a coordinate
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and define the predictable integrand

h_{k,j}(\tau):=\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)=\frac{\partial_{\theta_{j}}\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)}{\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)}.

By oracle positivity,
\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\geq\underline{\lambda}_{k}\geq\underline{\lambda}_{\min}
, and by the oracle
gradient bound in Assumption
S4.2
,

\|\nabla_{\theta}\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\|\leq S_{\infty}
a.e.; therefore

|h_{k,j}(\tau)|\leq\frac{\|\nabla_{\theta}\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\|}{\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)}\leq\frac{S_{\infty}}{\underline{\lambda}_{\min}}=:H.

On the window envelope event
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
we have
\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\leq\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\leq K_{\rm win}
,
so the predictable quadratic characteristic of
\int_{D}h_{k,j}\,dM_{k}^{\star}
satisfies

V_{k,j}:=\int_{D}h_{k,j}(\tau)^{2}\,\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\,d\tau\leq\int_{D}H^{2}\,\lambda_{k}^{\star}(\tau)\,d\tau\leq H^{2}\,K_{\rm win}\,|D|.

Apply Lemma
S4.34
(two-sided form) to the scalar martingale integral

X_{k,j}:=\int_{D}h_{k,j}\,dM_{k}^{\star}
. On
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-z_{\rm Fr}}
,

|X_{k,j}|\leq\frac{H}{3}z_{\rm Fr}+\sqrt{2V_{k,j}z_{\rm Fr}+\frac{H^{2}}{9}z_{\rm Fr}^{2}}\leq\frac{H}{3}z_{\rm Fr}+\sqrt{2H^{2}K_{\rm win}|D|\,z_{\rm Fr}+\frac{H^{2}}{9}z_{\rm Fr}^{2}}.

Using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
and
\sqrt{H^{2}}=H
gives

|X_{k,j}|\leq H\sqrt{2K_{\rm win}|D|\,z_{\rm Fr}}+\frac{2H}{3}z_{\rm Fr}.

Now union bound over the
2p
pairs
(k,j)
yields that, on
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
, with probability at least

1-4p\,e^{-z_{\rm Fr}}
the above bound holds simultaneously for all
k\in\{0,1\}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.
On this event, for each coordinate
j
we combine (
15
) with the triangle inequality:

\big|\partial_{\theta_{j}}\ell_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})\big|=\left|\sum_{k=0}^{1}X_{k,j}\right|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{1}|X_{k,j}|\leq 2H\sqrt{2K_{\rm win}|D|\,z_{\rm Fr}}+\frac{4H}{3}z_{\rm Fr}.

Dividing by
|D|
yields

\big|\partial_{\theta_{j}}f_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})\big|\leq 2H\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\rm win}z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}}+\frac{4H}{3}\frac{z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}.

Finally,
\|\nabla f\|\leq\sqrt{p}\,\max_{j}|\partial_{\theta_{j}}f|
, hence

\|\nabla f_{r^{\star}}(\theta^{\star})\|\leq\sqrt{p}\left(2H\sqrt{\frac{2K_{\rm win}z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}}+\frac{4H}{3}\frac{z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}\right)\leq\frac{8\sqrt{2p}\,S_{\infty}}{\underline{\lambda}_{\min}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{K_{\rm win}z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}}+\frac{z_{\rm Fr}}{|D|}\right),

which is the claimed bound with the stated choice of
C_{\rm sc}
.

Lemma S4.38

(Stochastic Fubini for
v
-interpolation)
.

Let
H(\tau,v)
be jointly measurable in
(\tau,v)\in D\times[0,1]
,
\mathcal{G}
-predictable in
\tau
for each
v
, and
\sup_{v\in[0,1]}\|H(\cdot,v)\|_{\infty}<\infty
. Suppose moreover that

\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\int_{D}H(\tau,v)^{2}\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}v\ <\ \infty.

Then

\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\int_{D}H(\tau,v)\mathrm{d}M^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}v\ =\ \int_{D}\!\Big(\int_{0}^{1}H(\tau,v)\mathrm{d}v\Big)\mathrm{d}M^{\star}(\tau)

almost surely, and the right-hand side is square-integrable
(
34
)
.

Lemma S4.39

(Deterministic Lipschitz bounds in
\theta
for common integrands)
.

Assume Assumptions
S4.2
and
S4.2
.
Then for every deterministic labelling
r
and for a.e.
\tau\in D
the following hold uniformly over
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\circ}
.

(a)

(Log-intensity Lipschitz.)
For each
k\in\{0,1\}
,

\big|\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)-\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta^{\prime})\big|\leq\frac{S_{\infty}}{\underline{\mu}_{\min}}\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|.

(b)

(Score-gradient Lipschitz.)
For each
k\in\{0,1\}
and coordinate
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,

\big|\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta)-\partial_{\theta_{j}}\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau;\theta^{\prime})\big|\leq\left(\frac{H_{\infty}}{\underline{\mu}_{\min}}+\frac{S_{\infty}^{2}}{\underline{\mu}_{\min}^{2}}\right)\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|.

(c)

(Interpolation-ratio Lipschitz.)
If
r^{\prime}
differs from
r
by a single flip and

\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}-\widehat{\lambda}^{r}
, then for every
v\in[0,1]
,

\left|\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau;\theta)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau;\theta)+v\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau;\theta)}-\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau;\theta^{\prime})}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau;\theta^{\prime})+v\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau;\theta^{\prime})}\right|\leq L_{\rm frac}\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|,

where one may take

L_{\rm frac}:=\frac{\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}}+\frac{B_{\infty}\big(2S_{\infty}+\overline{B}_{\infty}^{\Sigma}\big)}{\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}^{2}}.

Remark S4.40

(Uniform Freedman bounds along a blockwise update path)
.

We work under
P_{\theta^{\star}}
and apply union bounds over a finite collection of indices
(updates
m\leq\mathfrak{T}
, event indices/candidates within the relevant window, components, coordinates, and net points).
By Assumption
S4.2
, along Algorithm
10
the labels used inside any stochastic integral
on a window
D^{(m)}
are non-anticipating, so every label-induced intensity appearing as an integrand is
\mathcal{G}
-predictable
on that window.

Moreover, since
\theta^{(m)}
is
\mathcal{G}_{u_{m}}
-measurable, it is known throughout the subsequent block
(u_{m},u_{m+1}]
,
so treating
\theta^{(m)}
as fixed when applying Freedman inequality within that block is legitimate.
We obtain Freedman inequality bounds first at net points
\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}
and then extend deterministically to all

\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
using Lipschitz bounds in
\theta
.

Finally, because the deviation bounds are established uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
(via a finite net and a deterministic Lipschitz extension), they may be evaluated at the realized, data-dependent iterate
\theta^{(m)}
without any additional conditioning argument. Assumption
S4.2
(a) is used only to ensure that
\theta^{(m)}
is already known at time
u_{m}
, so the corresponding integrands are predictable on the subsequent block
(u_{m},u_{m+1}]
.

Lemma S4.41

(Predictability of forward cones)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a filtration satisfying the usual conditions, and let

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})
denote its predictable
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Let
T
be a
\mathcal{G}
-stopping time and let
X
be
\mathcal{G}_{T}
-measurable with values in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Fix
L,R>0
and a deterministic
u\geq 0
, and define the (random) forward cone

\displaystyle C^{[u]}(T,X):=

\displaystyle\Bigl(\{(t,x):T<t\leq u,\ t\leq T+L\}\Bigr)\cap\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times B_{R}(X)\bigr),

\displaystyle B_{R}(y):=

\displaystyle\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x-y\|\leq R\}.

Then the indicator
(\omega,t,x)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{C^{[u]}(T,X)}(t,x)
is

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-measurable (i.e.
\mathcal{G}
-predictable
as a space–time integrand).

Proof S4.42
.

Write
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Since
T
is a stopping time,
\{(\omega,t):T(\omega)<t\}\in\mathcal{P}
(e.g.
t\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{t>T\}
is adapted and
left-continuous).
Likewise,
T+L
is a stopping time, hence
\{(\omega,t):T(\omega)+L<t\}=\{t>T+L\}\in\mathcal{P}
, and therefore

\{(\omega,t):t\leq T+L\}\in\mathcal{P}
by closure under complements.
Also
(\Omega\times(0,u])\in\mathcal{P}
since
u
is deterministic.
Thus

A:=\{(\omega,t):T<t\leq u,\ t\leq T+L\}\in\mathcal{P}.

The map
(\omega,x)\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{\|x-X(\omega)\|\leq R\}
is
\sigma(X)\otimes\mathcal{B}
-measurable.
Since
X
is
\mathcal{G}_{T}
-measurable, for each rational
q\geq 0
and each Borel
B\in\mathcal{B}
,

\{X\in B\}\cap\{T\leq q\}\in\mathcal{G}_{q},

so by a monotone-class argument the restriction of
(\omega,x)\mapsto\mathbf{1}\{\|x-X(\omega)\|\leq R\}
to
\{T\leq q\}

is
\mathcal{G}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{B}
-measurable.

Now consider

E:=\{(\omega,t,x):T(\omega)<t,\ \|x-X(\omega)\|\leq R\}=\bigcup_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}}\Bigl(\{T\leq q\}\cap\{\|x-X\|\leq R\}\Bigr)\times(q,\infty).

Each set
\bigl(\{T\leq q\}\cap\{\|x-X\|\leq R\}\bigr)\times(q,\infty)
belongs to
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{B}

because
\{T\leq q\}\cap\{\|x-X\|\leq R\}\in\mathcal{G}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{P}
is generated by rectangles

F\times(s,t]
with
F\in\mathcal{G}_{s}
.
Hence
E\in\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{B}
.

Finally,

\mathbf{1}_{C^{[u]}(T,X)}(\omega,t,x)=\mathbf{1}_{A}(\omega,t)\,\mathbf{1}_{E}(\omega,t,x),

and since
A\in\mathcal{P}
and
E\in\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{B}
, we obtain

\mathbf{1}_{C^{[u]}(T,X)}\in\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{B}
.

S4.4
Penalized objective, per-flip bound, and E-step elimination

We first introduce our oracle aided penalized hard-EM algorithm. Fix an update index
m
and consider the cumulative (prefix) window
D^{(m)}=D^{[u_{m}]}=(t^{\ast},u_{m}]\times\mathcal{S}
. In Lemma
S4.50
and in the Freedman inequality bounds that follow, all stochastic integrals and event-sums are
understood to be taken over the active window
D^{(m)}
(equivalently, the likelihood is
\ell_{r}^{[u_{m}]}
and the
normalization is by
|D^{(m)}|
).
This is exactly the regime where Assumption
S4.2
guarantees
\mathcal{G}
-predictability of the integrands. Any dependence on the data-dependent iterate
\theta^{(m)}
is handled by establishing Freedman inequality bounds uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
(via a finite net and deterministic Lipschitz extension), as detailed in
Remark

S4.40
. The special case
m=\mathfrak{T}
corresponds to the full window
u_{m}=T
. Given
b>0
and
\alpha>0
, define the oracle-aided penalized objective on a prefix window
D^{[u]}
as
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{[u]}(r,\theta)
in (
16
). In this section, at update
m
we work with
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{[u_{m}]}
on the active window
D^{(m)}
.

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{[u]}(r,\theta):=\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)-\alpha K_{\rm win}\cdot\#\Big\{\gamma_{i}:\ t_{i}\leq u,\ \gamma_{i}\in S_{\theta}^{+}(b)\cup S_{\theta}^{-}(b),\ r_{i}=\text{minority}(\gamma_{i};\theta,b)\Big\}.

(16)

We also write
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(r,\theta):=\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{[T]}(r,\theta)
for the full-window objective.

We now describe the predictable (greedy) E-step under the blockwise schedule

t^{\ast}=u_{0}<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{\mathfrak{T}}=T
.
At update
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}-1\}
we freeze the parameter
\theta^{(m)}

(which is
\mathcal{G}_{u_{m}}
-measurable by Assumption
S4.2
(a))
and assign labels only for events in the next block
D_{m+1}=(u_{m},u_{m+1}]\times\mathcal{S}
,
sequentially in event time, while keeping all past labels fixed.

Specifically, for each event
\gamma_{i}=(t_{i},x_{i})
with
u_{m}<t_{i}\leq u_{m+1}
,
having already fixed
r^{(m+1)}_{1:i-1}
(which includes all labels from previous blocks and earlier events in the current block),
set

\displaystyle r_{i}^{(m+1)}\in\argmax_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Big\{

\displaystyle\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r^{(m+1)}}(\gamma_{i};\theta^{(m)})

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha K_{\rm win}\,\mathbbm{1}\!\left\{\gamma_{i}\in S^{+}_{\theta^{(m)}}(b)\cup S^{-}_{\theta^{(m)}}(b),\ k=\mathrm{minority}(\gamma_{i};\theta^{(m)},b)\right\}\Big\}.

(17)

with a deterministic tie-breaking rule (and at decisive events ties are broken in favor of the non-minority label).
For all indices
j
with
t_{j}\leq u_{m}
we set
r_{j}^{(m+1)}:=r_{j}^{(m)}
(labels are not revised).

Algorithm 1 (Analyzed operator): Predictable blockwise penalized hard-EM.

Input:
deterministic partition
t^{\ast}=u_{0}<\cdots<u_{\mathfrak{T}}=T
; warm start
\theta^{(0)}\in\Theta_{\rm sc}
;
threshold
b\geq b_{\rm th}
; penalty
\alpha>0
.

Initialize:

r^{(0)}
(arbitrary; no labels are fixed before the first block).

For
m=0,1,\dots,\mathfrak{T}-1
:

(E-step on the next block
D_{m+1}
)
Assign labels for events with
u_{m}<t_{i}\leq u_{m+1}
sequentially by
(
S4.4
), keeping labels with
t_{j}\leq u_{m}
fixed.

(M-step at the block end)
Update the parameter using the cumulative window
D^{(m+1)}=(t^{\ast},u_{m+1}]\times\mathcal{S}
:

\theta^{(m+1)}:=\hat{\theta}_{\Theta_{\rm sc}}^{[u_{m+1}]}\!\big(r^{(m+1)}\big)\in\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm sc}}\ell_{r^{(m+1)}}^{[u_{m+1}]}(\theta).

Output:

\theta^{(\mathfrak{T})}
and the (progressively assigned) labels
r^{(\mathfrak{T})}
on
D
.

Figure 10:
Predictable blockwise penalized hard-EM operator used in the analysis. The practical SEM implementation may include additional offline smoothing passes, but the theoretical argument only requires the time-respecting operator displayed here.

Lemma S4.43

(Non-anticipation of the blockwise greedy E-step)
.

Fix
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}-1\}
and suppose:
(i)
\theta^{(m)}
is
\mathcal{G}_{u_{m}}
-measurable, and
(ii) the current labels
r^{(m)}
satisfy Assumption
S4.2
(b) up to time
u_{m}
. Construct
r^{(m+1)}
by keeping all labels with
t_{j}\leq u_{m}
fixed and assigning labels for events
\gamma_{i}
with
u_{m}<t_{i}\leq u_{m+1}
sequentially by the greedy rule (
S4.4
), using a deterministic tie-breaking rule. Then
r^{(m+1)}
satisfies Assumption
S4.2
(b) up to time
u_{m+1}
.

Proof S4.44
.

For indices
i
with
t_{i}\leq u_{m}
we have
r_{i}^{(m+1)}=r_{i}^{(m)}
, hence
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurability holds by the
induction (progressive) hypothesis in Assumption
S4.2
(b).

Now consider the events in the new block
D_{m+1}
and list them in increasing time order.
We argue by induction over this within-block ordering.
For the first such event
\gamma_{i}
(smallest
t_{i}
with
u_{m}<t_{i}\leq u_{m+1}
), the objective in (
S4.4
)
depends only on:
(i) the observed history
\mathcal{H}_{t_{i}-}\subset\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
,
(ii) already-fixed past labels
r^{(m+1)}_{1:i-1}
(which are
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable by construction),
and (iii)
\theta^{(m)}
, which is
\mathcal{G}_{u_{m}}
-measurable and hence
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable because
u_{m}<t_{i}

and
(\mathcal{G}_{t})
is increasing.
With deterministic tie-breaking,
r_{i}^{(m+1)}
is therefore
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable.

Assume the claim holds for all earlier events in
D_{m+1}
. For the next event
\gamma_{i^{\prime}}
in the block,
the greedy objective depends on
\mathcal{H}_{t_{i^{\prime}}-}
, the already-chosen labels in the block,
and
\theta^{(m)}
, all of which are
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i^{\prime}}}
-measurable. Deterministic tie-breaking again yields that

r_{i^{\prime}}^{(m+1)}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i^{\prime}}}
-measurable. This closes the induction.

Remark S4.45

(Why the blockwise timing condition is needed)
.

If one computes
\theta^{(m)}
from the entire offline window
D=(t^{\ast},T]\times\mathcal{S}
and then uses it to
assign labels to early events
\gamma_{i}
with
t_{i}
close to
t^{\ast}
, then
r_{i}^{(m+1)}
generally depends on future events
through
\theta^{(m)}
and need not be
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable. This breaks Assumption
S4.2

(and hence
\mathcal{G}
-predictability of the label-induced intensities inside martingale integrals).
The deterministic blockwise schedule in Algorithm
10
enforces the required timing:

\theta^{(m)}
is computed using data only up to
u_{m}
and is therefore known when labelling any event in
(u_{m},u_{m+1}]
.

Lemma
S4.43
shows that, under Assumption
S4.2
(a), the blockwise sequential construction produces labels that satisfy the progressive
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurability in Assumption
S4.2
(b). In case of a tie at a decisive event
\gamma_{i}\in S^{+}_{\theta^{(m)}}(b)\cup S^{-}_{\theta^{(m)}}(b)
we break ties by choosing the non-minority label. Given
r^{(m+1)}
, the M-step (localized for the analysis under Assumption
S4.2
) at the block end
u_{m+1}
is

\theta^{(m+1)}\ :=\ \hat{\theta}_{r^{(m+1)}}^{[u_{m+1}]}\ \in\ \arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta_{\rm sc}}\ell_{r^{(m+1)}}^{[u_{m+1}]}(\theta),\qquad\Theta_{\rm sc}:=\Theta_{\circ}\cap B(\theta^{\star},r_{\rm sc}).

We do not assume the M-step maximiser is unique. Whenever
\arg\max_{\theta\in K}\ell_{r}^{[u]}(\theta)
is set-valued, the algorithm may choose any maximiser (e.g. returned by a numerical routine). All bounds below hold uniformly for
every such choice. For notational convenience we fix a selection
\hat{\theta}_{K}^{[u]}(r)\in\operatorname{ArgMax}_{K}^{[u]}(r)
and write
\theta^{(m+1)}:=\hat{\theta}_{\Theta_{\rm sc}}^{[u_{m+1}]}(r^{(m+1)})
.

This ball localization is imposed only for the analysis, to ensure that the selected M-step maximiser lies in the region where Assumption
S4.2
controls the curvature of
f_{r^{\star}}
. Once the two-line recursion holds with
\rho<1
and the statistical floor is sufficiently small, Lemma
S4.64
implies that the iterates remain in a strict sub-neighbourhood of
\theta^{\star}
(hence in
\operatorname{int}(\Theta_{\rm sc})
), so the additional ball constraint does not bind along the contraction path.

We notate the update count and subsequent union bounds as
\mathfrak{T}=\lceil C_{\rm it}\log|D|\rceil
as in
Section

S4.2
. Under Algorithm
10
, labels are assigned progressively and never revised.
Hence each event
\gamma_{i}
contributes at most one label assignment over the entire update path.
On the activity event
E_{\rm act}
of
Section

S4.2
we therefore have the total update count bound

M_{\rm flips}\ \leq\ N(D)\ \leq\ (1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|.

(18)

(Algorithm
10
assigns each event label once, progressively, and never revisits past events. Any additional smoothing passes used in practice are outside the analysed operator.)

We next introduce the master high-probability event.
Fix
z_{\rm Fr}>0
and define

E_{\mathrm{master}}(z_{\rm Fr}):=E_{\rm act}\cap\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)\cap E^{\rm align}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm Lip}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm sc}_{|D|}\cap E^{\nabla}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm mass}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm Fr}_{|D|}(z_{\rm Fr}),

where
E^{\rm Fr}_{|D|}(z_{\rm Fr})
denotes the intersection of all Freedman deviation events (with level
z_{\rm Fr}
)
invoked in Lemma
S4.46
(ii) (per-flip martingales and M-step anchor gradient-coordinate martingales,
uniformly over
m\leq\mathfrak{T}
, the relevant event indices, components, and net points).
On
E_{\mathrm{master}}(z_{\rm Fr})
, all inequalities used in the subsequent E-step/M-step arguments hold simultaneously.

Moreover,
\Pr(E_{\mathrm{master}}(z_{\rm Fr})^{c})
is controlled by a finite union bound over the constituent events
(and over the finite family of Freedman deviations at level
z_{\rm Fr}
), yielding an overall failure probability
of order
|D|^{-\eta^{\prime\prime}}
for some
\eta^{\prime\prime}>0
. For notational convenience, set

E_{\rm main}:=E_{\rm act}\cap\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)\cap E^{\rm align}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm Lip}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm sc}_{|D|}\cap E^{\nabla}_{|D|}\cap E^{\rm mass}_{|D|},\qquad n_{*}(|D|)=\lceil c_{1}\log|D|\rceil.

Lemma S4.46

(Master high-probability event)
.

Assume
Sections

S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
and
S4.2
. There exists
\eta^{\prime\prime}>0
such that, for all large
|D|
, with probability at least
1-|D|^{-\eta^{\prime\prime}}
, the following hold simultaneously:

(i)

E_{\rm main}
occurs;

(ii)

For every update index
m\leq\mathfrak{T}
, every event index
j\leq N^{(m)}
(i.e. with
t_{j}\leq u_{m}
),
every selected component
k\in\{0,1\}
, and every net point
\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}
,
the Freedman inequality bounds with the same parameter
z_{\rm Fr}
hold simultaneously for the scalar martingales used in Lemma
S4.50
(on the active window
D^{(m)}
). Moreover, for every update index
m\in\{0,\dots,\mathfrak{T}-1\}
(with
u=u_{m+1}
and
r=r^{(m+1)}
), and for every gradient coordinate used in the Assumption
S4.2
verification at the M-step anchor
\hat{\theta}_{r}^{[u]}=\hat{\theta}_{r^{(m+1)}}^{[u_{m+1}]}
, the corresponding Freedman inequality bounds (with the same
z_{\rm Fr}
) hold simultaneously.

Proof S4.47
.

We establish the result in two steps: first, a union bound over a finite net to handle the martingale noise; second, a deterministic Lipschitz extension to cover the entire parameter space
\Theta_{\circ}
.

Recall the fixed, deterministic
\varepsilon_{\rm net}
-net
\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}\subset\Theta_{\circ}
from
Section

S4.2
. For concreteness one may take
\varepsilon_{\rm net}:=|D|^{-2}
(which satisfies
\varepsilon_{\rm net}\leq|D|^{-1}
for
|D|\geq 1
, which is eventually true). Since
p
is fixed,
|\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}|\leq(C\operatorname{diam}(\Theta_{\circ})/\varepsilon_{\rm net})^{p}
, so
\log N_{\rm net}=O(\log|D|)
.
Item (i) is exactly the event
E_{\rm main}
, whose failure probability is controlled by a finite union bound using the
tail bounds in
Sections

S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
,
S4.2
and
S4.2
. For (ii), we consider the collection of all scalar martingales evaluated by the algorithm at update index
m
, event index
j
, component
k
, and parameter
\theta
. By Assumption
S4.2
, for any fixed
\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}
, the integrand defining these martingales depends only on the history and is
\mathcal{G}
-predictable on the relevant cumulative window.

Let
N_{\rm total}
denote the total number of (two-sided) Freedman inequality tail events that we union bound over. On
E_{\rm act}
, the number of prefix event indices we union over is at most
\sum_{m=1}^{\mathfrak{T}}N^{(m)}\leq\mathfrak{T}\,N(D)\leq(1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|\,\mathfrak{T}
. Each candidate contributes at most
(p+2)
martingales (two scalar terms in Lemma
S4.50
and
p
gradient coordinates), and we take a two-sided deviation bound. Therefore, for an absolute constant
c_{0}
(absorbing these fixed factors, unions over update indices, and any ancillary Freedman inequality calls included in the master union bound),

N_{\rm total}\ \leq\ c_{0}\,(p+2)\,N_{\rm net}\,(1+\eta_{\rm act})C_{\rm act}|D|\,\mathfrak{T}.

The parameter
z_{\rm Fr}
in (
12
) is chosen so that
e^{-z_{\rm Fr}}
dominates
N_{\rm total}^{-1}
(up to the extra
|D|^{-\eta_{x}}
factor), making the union bound over all
N_{\rm total}
Freedman events polynomially small in
|D|
. Applying
Lemma

S4.34
and summing the failure probabilities yields that, with probability at least
1-|D|^{-\eta^{\prime\prime}}
, the Freedman inequality bounds hold simultaneously for all
\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}
and all algorithmic steps.

We now extend to
\Theta_{\circ}
via Lipschitz continuity. Working on the intersection of the activity event
E_{\rm act}
, and the window envelope event
\Omega_{n_{*}}(L,R)
, consider an arbitrary
\theta\in\Theta_{\circ}
. Let
\theta_{g}\in\mathcal{N}_{\rm net}
be the closest net point, so
\|\theta-\theta_{g}\|\leq\varepsilon_{\rm net}
. Let
X(\theta)=\int_{D}H(\tau;\theta)\mathrm{d}M^{\star}(\tau)
be any of the generic martingale terms (e.g., from
Lemma

S4.50
or the gradient in Assumption
S4.2
). We decompose the value at
\theta
as:

|X(\theta)|\leq|X(\theta_{g})|+|X(\theta)-X(\theta_{g})|.

The first term is bounded by the grid result in the above union bound. For the second term, we use the decomposition
\mathrm{d}M^{\star}=\mathrm{d}N-\lambda^{\star}\mathrm{d}\tau
:

|X(\theta)-X(\theta_{g})|=\left|\int_{D}(H(\tau;\theta)-H(\tau;\theta_{g}))\mathrm{d}M^{\star}\right|\leq\int_{D}\Delta_{H}(\tau)\mathrm{d}N(\tau)+\int_{D}\Delta_{H}(\tau)\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau,

where
\Delta_{H}(\tau)=|H(\tau;\theta)-H(\tau;\theta_{g})|
.
By Lemma
S4.39
(and the margins in Assumption
S4.2
), the relevant integrands
H(\tau;\cdot)
are Lipschitz in
\theta
on
\Theta_{\circ}
with a deterministic constant
L_{H}<\infty
(dependent on model constants and
K_{\rm win}
, but independent of
|D|
). Thus
\Delta_{H}(\tau)\leq L_{H}\varepsilon_{\rm net}
.
Substituting this bound:

|X(\theta)-X(\theta_{g})|\leq L_{H}\varepsilon_{\rm net}\big(N(D)+\Lambda^{\star}(D)\big).

On
E_{\rm act}
we have
N(D)=O(|D|)
, and on
\Omega_{n_{*}}
we have
\Lambda^{\star}(D)=\int_{D}\lambda^{\star}\leq K_{\rm win}|D|
. Since we chose
\varepsilon_{\rm net}=|D|^{-2}
, the discretization error is

O\!\left(L_{H}\,\varepsilon_{\rm net}\,(N(D)+\Lambda^{\star}(D))\right)=O_{\mathbb{P}}\!\left(\frac{L_{H}\,(1+K_{\rm win})}{|D|}\right).

Since we chose
\varepsilon_{\rm net}=|D|^{-2}
, this discretization error is
O_{\mathbb{P}}\!\big(L_{H}(1+K_{\rm win})/|D|\big)
. When the corresponding term appears with the
|D|^{-1}
normalization (as in
f_{r}=|D|^{-1}\ell_{r}
), the discretization contribution is
O_{\mathbb{P}}\!\big(L_{H}(1+K_{\rm win})/|D|^{2}\big)
, hence negligible.

S4.4.1
Per-flip decomposition and explicit constants

We adopt
\sigma_{j}=+1
for a
0\!\to\!1
flip and
\sigma_{j}=-1
for a
1\!\to\!0
flip at
\zeta=\gamma_{j}
. Recall
\underline{\mu}_{\min}=\min\{\underline{\mu}_{0},\underline{\mu}_{1}\}
and
\underline{\mu}_{\Sigma}
from Assumption
S4.2
.

Lemma S4.48

(Single flip preserves non-anticipation on the active prefix)
.

Fix
m\leq\mathfrak{T}
and work on the active window
D^{(m)}
(as per the convention above).
Let
r=(r_{i})_{i\geq 1}
be a (possibly random) labelling such that
r_{i}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable for every

i\leq N^{(m)}
.
Fix an index
j\leq N^{(m)}
and define the single-flip labelling
r^{(\gamma_{j})}
by

r^{(\gamma_{j})}_{i}=\begin{cases}r_{i},&i\neq j,\\
1-r_{j},&i=j.\end{cases}

Then
r^{(\gamma_{j})}_{i}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable for every
i\leq N^{(m)}
.

Proof S4.49
.

For
i\neq j
,
r^{(\gamma_{j})}_{i}=r_{i}
is
\mathcal{G}_{t_{i}}
-measurable by assumption.
For
i=j
,
r^{(\gamma_{j})}_{j}=1-r_{j}
is a measurable function of
r_{j}
and hence
\mathcal{G}_{t_{j}}
-measurable.

Lemma S4.50

(Per-flip identity and bounds for
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}
)
.

Assume
Sections

S4.2
,
S4.2
and
S4.2
, and Assumption
S4.2
along the update path, and fix
m\leq\mathfrak{T}
. Throughout this lemma, all likelihoods, event-sums, and stochastic integrals are taken over the active prefix window

D^{(m)}=(t^{\ast},u_{m}]\times\mathcal{S}
(cf. the active-window convention at the start of this section).
Fix
j\leq N^{(m)}
, and let
r^{\prime}=r^{(\zeta)}
be a single flip at
\zeta=\gamma_{j}
. By Lemma
S4.48
,
r^{\prime}
is also non-anticipating, hence all label-induced intensities below are
\mathcal{G}
-predictable. Then

\ell_{r^{\prime}}^{[u_{m}]}(\theta)-\ell_{r}^{[u_{m}]}(\theta)=\sigma_{j}\,\tilde{s}_{\theta}(\zeta;r)+\mathcal{M}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{2}+\mathcal{R}_{\rm sel},

(19)

where, for
v\in[0,1]
, writing
\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}=\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}-\widehat{\lambda}^{r}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\int_{D^{(m)}}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)+v\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}\mathrm{d}M^{\star}(\tau)\mathrm{d}v,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\int_{D^{(m)}}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)+v\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}\big(\lambda^{\star}(\tau)-\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\big)\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}v,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\int_{D^{(m)}}\frac{v(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau))^{2}}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)+v\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}v\ \ (\geq 0).

and the selection correction is the (pathwise) sum over events

\mathcal{R}_{\rm sel}:=\sum_{i:\,\gamma_{i}\in D^{(m)}}\Big(\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}_{r_{i}}\!\big(\gamma_{i};\theta\big)-\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r}_{r_{i}}\!\big(\gamma_{i};\theta\big)-\big(\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}(\gamma_{i};\theta)-\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\gamma_{i};\theta)\big)\Big),

The decomposition (
19
) is purely algebraic and holds path-wise, without any probabilistic event.
The quantitative bounds (
20
)–(
22
) stated below hold on the master event of Lemma
S4.46
, since they use only the lower margins in Assumption
S4.2
, the window envelope in Assumption
S4.2
, and the Freedman inequalities included in that master event.

Proof S4.51
.

Fix two labellings
r,r^{\prime}
that differ at exactly one index
j
(the “flipped” event), and write

\Delta\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau):=\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r^{\prime}}(\tau)-\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau),\qquad\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau):=\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}(\tau)-\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau),

where
\widehat{\lambda}^{r}=\widehat{\lambda}_{0}^{r}+\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{r}
. For the remainder of this proof, we write
D:=D^{(m)}
for brevity. For
v\in[0,1]
also define the interpolated intensity

\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau):=\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)+v\,\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau).

Using the decomposition

\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}\int_{D}\log\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau)\,dN_{k}^{r}(\tau)=\int_{D}\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\,dN(\tau)+\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}\int_{D}\log\!\Big(\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}\Big)\,dN_{k}^{r}(\tau),

we may write the objective (restricted to
D
) as a sum of a total-intensity log-likelihood term and a selection term. Hence

\ell_{r^{\prime}}^{[u_{m}]}(\theta)-\ell_{r}^{[u_{m}]}(\theta)=\Big[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r)\Big]+\Big[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sel}}(r^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sel}}(r)\Big],

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r):=\int_{D}\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r}\,dN-\int_{D}\widehat{\lambda}^{r}\,d\tau
.

For the log term, apply the identity

\log a-\log b=(a-b)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{b+v(a-b)}\,dv\qquad(a,b>0),

pointwise with
a=\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}(\tau)
and
b=\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)
to obtain

\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}(\tau)-\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,dv.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\big(\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r^{\prime}}-\log\widehat{\lambda}^{r}\big)\,dN-\int_{D}\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,dN(\tau)\,dv-\int_{D}\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\,d\tau.

Now decompose
dN=dM^{\star}+\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\,d\tau
to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,dM^{\star}(\tau)\,dv}_{:=\,\mathcal{M}_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,\lambda^{\star}(\tau)\,d\tau\,dv-\int_{D}\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{1}+\int_{0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\big(\lambda^{\star}(\tau)-\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)\big)\,d\tau\,dv.

By Lemma
S4.38
(stochastic Fubini), we may interchange the
v
-integration and the martingale integral and write

\mathcal{M}_{1}=\int_{D}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,dv\right)\,dM^{\star}(\tau),

so
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is a well-defined martingale integral.

Using
\lambda^{\star}-\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}=(\lambda^{\star}-\widehat{\lambda}^{r})-v\,\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}
, the last integral becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\big(\lambda^{\star}(\tau)-\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\big)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,d\tau\,dv-\int_{0}^{1}\int_{D}\frac{v\big(\Delta\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)\big)^{2}}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r,v}(\tau)}\,d\tau\,dv

\displaystyle\qquad=:\mathcal{D}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{2}.

Thus
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tot}}(r)=\mathcal{M}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{1}-\mathcal{D}_{2}
, matching the lemma’s definitions.

We now examine the selection part and the single-flip term. Write the selection term as an event-sum:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sel}}(r)=\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}\int_{D}\log\!\Big(\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{k}^{r}(\tau)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\tau)}\Big)\,dN_{k}^{r}(\tau)=\sum_{i:\,\gamma_{i}\in D}\log\!\Big(\frac{\widehat{\lambda}_{r_{i}}^{r}(\gamma_{i})}{\widehat{\lambda}^{r}(\gamma_{i})}\Big).

Since
r
and
r^{\prime}
differ only at
j
, split the difference into the contribution from
i\neq j
and the contribution at index
j
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sel}}(r^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sel}}(r)
